class \([a]_n\) is a unit (Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff}), so
\([a]_n^{\varphi(n)} = [1]_n\), which is the first congruence. For
\(n = p\) prime, \(\varphi(p) = p - 1\)
(Example~\ref{intmod:exa:totient}), and \(p \nmid a\) gives
\(\gcd(a,p) = 1\), yielding the second congruence. Multiplying it by
\(a\) gives \(a^p \equiv a \pmod{p}\); and when \(p \mid a\) both sides
are congruent to \(0\), so the last congruence holds for all \(a\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{groups:rem-euler-cryptorole}
The congruences of Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat} are proved
here, once; \cref{sec:numbertheory} restates them under the heading
\emph{Fermat's little theorem and Euler's theorem} and develops their
cryptographic role there, citing this proof rather than repeating it.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}\label{groups:cor-prime-cyclic}
Every group \(G\) of prime order \(p\) is cyclic, hence isomorphic to
\(\Z/p\Z\), and has no proper nontrivial subgroups.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Pick \(a \in G\) with \(a \neq e\) (possible since \(p \ge 2\)). The
subgroup \(\langle a\rangle\) has at least two elements, and by
Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-lagrange} its order divides \(p\); a divisor of
\(p\) exceeding \(1\) equals \(p\), so \(\langle a\rangle = G\) and \(G\)
is cyclic. The isomorphism with \(\Z/p\Z\) is
Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-cyclic-classification} below. Finally, any
subgroup has order \(1\) or \(p\) by Lagrange, i.e.\ is \(\{e\}\) or
\(G\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Quotients of abelian groups}\label{groups:sub:quotients}

Cosets do more than count: for an abelian group they themselves form a
group. This single construction underlies the quotient rings of
\cref{sec:ringsfields} and, through them, \(\Z/n\Z\) and the polynomial
quotients used throughout the series; it is all of quotient theory the
volume requires.

\begin{proposition}[Quotient of an abelian
group]\label{groups:prop-quotient-abelian}
Let \(A\) be an abelian group, written additively, and let \(H \le A\).
The operation
\[
(a + H) + (b + H) = (a + b) + H
\]
on the coset set \(A/H\) is well defined and makes \(A/H\) an abelian
group---the \emph{quotient group} of \(A\) by \(H\)---with identity
\(H = 0 + H\) and inverse \(-(a+H) = (-a) + H\). If \(A\) is finite,
then
\[
\abs{A/H} = [A : H] = \frac{\abs{A}}{\abs{H}} .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The only point of substance is that the operation is well defined: its
output is stated in terms of chosen representatives \(a\) and \(b\), and
we must check that replacing them by other representatives of the same
cosets leaves the result unchanged. Suppose then that
\(a + H = a' + H\) and \(b + H = b' + H\). By
Lemma~\ref{groups:lem-cosets-partition}(2), written additively, this
means \(a' - a \in H\) and \(b' - b \in H\). Then
\[
(a' + b') - (a + b) = (a' - a) + (b' - b) \in H ,
\]
since \(H\) is closed under addition; applying
Lemma~\ref{groups:lem-cosets-partition}(2) once more,
\((a'+b') + H = (a+b) + H\). The operation is well defined. This
representative-independence check is the template for every coset-wise
construction in the volume; it recurs, in the same words, for
quotient-ring multiplication in \cref{sec:ringsfields}.

The axioms now pass coset-wise from \(A\): for associativity,
\[
\bigl((a+H) + (b+H)\bigr) + (c+H) = \bigl((a+b)+c\bigr) + H
= \bigl(a+(b+c)\bigr) + H = (a+H) + \bigl((b+H) + (c+H)\bigr);
\]
commutativity follows the same way from commutativity in \(A\); the
coset \(0 + H = H\) is an identity; and \((-a) + H\) is an inverse of
\(a + H\). Hence \(A/H\) is an abelian group. When \(A\) is finite, the
number of cosets is \([A:H] = \abs{A}/\abs{H}\) by
Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-lagrange}---the same partition-into-equal-blocks
count as before.
\end{proof}

The construction is not new to this volume in disguise: for
\(A = (\Z,+)\) and \(H = n\Z\), Definition~\ref{intmod:def:residueclass}
exhibits each residue class as \([a]_n = a + n\Z\), a coset of
\(n\Z \le \Z\), and the coset addition above is exactly addition of
classes. The notation \(\Z/n\Z\), introduced in \cref{sec:intmod} as a
bare name for the set of classes, is thus an instance of the general
quotient notation \(A/H\), and \((\Z/n\Z,+)\) is the quotient group
\(\Z/n\Z\) in the sense just constructed.

\begin{remark}\label{groups:rem-quotient-nonabelian}
For a non-abelian group \(G\) the same construction succeeds exactly for
those subgroups \(N \le G\) whose left and right cosets coincide,
\(gN = Ng\) for all \(g \in G\); these are the \emph{normal} subgroups.
Commutativity makes every subgroup of an abelian group normal, which is
why no extra hypothesis was needed in
Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-quotient-abelian}. The present volume
deliberately needs only the abelian case, and we do not develop the
general theory.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Homomorphisms and isomorphisms}\label{groups:sub:homs}

Groups are compared through the maps that respect their operations.

\begin{definition}[Group homomorphism]\label{groups:def-hom}
Let \((G,\ast)\) and \((G',\circ)\) be groups. A function
\(\phi \colon G \to G'\) is a \emph{group homomorphism} if
\[
\phi(a \ast b) = \phi(a) \circ \phi(b)
\qquad\text{for all } a, b \in G .
\]
The set of all homomorphisms from \(G\) to \(G'\) is denoted
\(\Hom(G, G')\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{groups:prop-hom-basic}
Let \(\phi \colon G \to G'\) be a homomorphism, and write \(e, e'\) for
the identities of \(G\) and \(G'\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\phi(e) = e'\).
\item \(\phi(a^{-1}) = \phi(a)^{-1}\) for all \(a \in G\).
\item \(\phi(a^n) = \phi(a)^n\) for all \(a \in G\) and \(n \in \Z\).
\item The image \(\phi(G)\) is a subgroup of \(G'\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) From \(e = e \ast e\) we get
\(\phi(e) = \phi(e) \circ \phi(e)\); cancelling \(\phi(e)\) in \(G'\)
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-unique-id-inv}(3)) gives \(e' = \phi(e)\).

(2) Applying \(\phi\) to \(a \ast a^{-1} = e = a^{-1} \ast a\) and using
(1),
\[
\phi(a) \circ \phi(a^{-1}) = e' = \phi(a^{-1}) \circ \phi(a),
\]
so \(\phi(a^{-1})\) is an inverse of \(\phi(a)\); by uniqueness of
inverses, \(\phi(a^{-1}) = \phi(a)^{-1}\).

(3) For \(n \ge 0\), induct: the case \(n = 0\) is (1), and
\(\phi(a^{n+1}) = \phi(a^n \ast a) = \phi(a)^n \circ \phi(a)
= \phi(a)^{n+1}\). For \(n < 0\), write \(n = -m\) with \(m > 0\) and
combine with (2):
\(\phi(a^{-m}) = \phi((a^{-1})^m) = \phi(a^{-1})^m
= (\phi(a)^{-1})^m = \phi(a)^{-m}\).

(4) The image is nonempty, containing \(e' = \phi(e)\). For
\(x = \phi(a)\) and \(y = \phi(b)\) in \(\phi(G)\),
\[
x \circ y^{-1} = \phi(a) \circ \phi(b)^{-1} = \phi(a \ast b^{-1})
\in \phi(G),
\]
using (2); the one-step subgroup test
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-subgroup-test}) concludes.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Kernel and image]\label{groups:def-kernel}
The \emph{kernel} and \emph{image} of a homomorphism
\(\phi \colon G \to G'\) are
\[
\ker(\phi) = \{\, a \in G : \phi(a) = e' \,\} = \phi^{-1}(\{e'\}),
\qquad
\img(\phi) = \phi(G) = \{\, \phi(a) : a \in G \,\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{groups:prop-kernel-injective}
For any homomorphism \(\phi \colon G \to G'\), the kernel is a subgroup,
\(\ker(\phi) \le G\), and \(\phi\) is injective if and only if
\(\ker(\phi) = \{e\}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The kernel contains \(e\) by
Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-hom-basic}(1), so it is nonempty; and for
\(a, b \in \ker(\phi)\),
\[
\phi(a b^{-1}) = \phi(a) \circ \phi(b)^{-1} = e' \circ (e')^{-1} = e',
\]
so \(a b^{-1} \in \ker(\phi)\); the one-step test gives
\(\ker(\phi) \le G\).

If \(\phi\) is injective and \(\phi(a) = e' = \phi(e)\), then \(a = e\);
the kernel is trivial. Conversely, suppose \(\ker(\phi) = \{e\}\) and
\(\phi(a) = \phi(b)\). Then
\(\phi(a b^{-1}) = \phi(a) \circ \phi(b)^{-1} = e'\), so
\(a b^{-1} \in \ker(\phi) = \{e\}\), forcing \(a b^{-1} = e\), i.e.\
\(a = b\).
\end{proof}

Kernels are exactly the subgroups by which one may quotient: for abelian
\(G\), Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-quotient-abelian} forms
\(G/\ker(\phi)\) directly, and in general the kernel satisfies the
normality condition of Remark~\ref{groups:rem-quotient-nonabelian}---for
\(n \in \ker(\phi)\) and \(g \in G\), one computes
\(\phi(g n g^{-1}) = \phi(g) \circ e' \circ \phi(g)^{-1} = e'\), so
conjugating the kernel by \(g\) lands back in the kernel, whence
\(g \ker(\phi) = \ker(\phi)\, g\).

\begin{definition}[Isomorphism]\label{groups:def-iso}
An \emph{isomorphism} is a bijective homomorphism
\(\phi \colon G \to G'\); when one exists, the groups are
\emph{isomorphic}, written \(G \cong G'\). An isomorphism from \(G\) to
itself is an \emph{automorphism}. An injective homomorphism is a
\emph{monomorphism}; a surjective one is an \emph{epimorphism}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{groups:prop-iso-inverse}
The set-theoretic inverse of an isomorphism is an isomorphism, and
\(\cong\) is an equivalence relation on any collection of groups.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\phi \colon G \to G'\) be an isomorphism, with inverse function
\(\phi^{-1}\) (which exists since \(\phi\) is a bijection,
Theorem~\ref{sets:thm:inverse}). For \(x, y \in G'\), write
\(x = \phi(a)\) and \(y = \phi(b)\); then
\[
\phi^{-1}(x \circ y) = \phi^{-1}\bigl(\phi(a \ast b)\bigr) = a \ast b
= \phi^{-1}(x) \ast \phi^{-1}(y),
\]
so \(\phi^{-1}\) is a homomorphism, and it is a bijection; hence an
isomorphism. For the equivalence relation: reflexivity is witnessed by
the identity automorphism \(\id_G\); symmetry by the inverse just
constructed; and transitivity because a composite of homomorphisms is a
homomorphism (apply the defining property twice) and a composite of
bijections is a bijection.
\end{proof}

Isomorphic groups are ``the same group'' for every group-theoretic
purpose: any property expressible in terms of the operation alone---
orders of elements, the lattice of subgroups, commutativity---transfers
across an isomorphism. One therefore classifies groups \emph{up to
isomorphism}, and the classification of cyclic groups is the model
result of this kind: up to isomorphism there is exactly one cyclic group
of each order.

\begin{theorem}[Classification of cyclic
groups]\label{groups:thm-cyclic-classification}
Every cyclic group is isomorphic to exactly one of the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the infinite cyclic group \((\Z,+)\), if it is infinite;
\item the finite cyclic group \((\Z/n\Z,+)\), if it has order \(n\).
\end{enumerate}
In particular, two cyclic groups are isomorphic if and only if they have
the same order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let \(G = \langle g\rangle\) and define
\[
\phi \colon \Z \to G, \qquad \phi(k) = g^k .
\]
The exponent law \(g^{k+l} = g^k g^l\) says exactly that \(\phi\) is a
homomorphism from \((\Z,+)\) to \(G\), and \(\phi\) is surjective
because every element of \(G\) is a power of \(g\).

\emph{Case 1: \(g\) has infinite order.} Distinct exponents give
distinct powers (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-cyclic-order}(1)), so
\(\phi\) is injective, hence a bijective homomorphism, and
\(\Z \cong G\).

\emph{Case 2: \(\ord(g) = n\).} By
Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(1),
\[
\ker(\phi) = \{\, k \in \Z : g^k = e \,\} = n\Z .
\]
Define the induced map
\[
\bar\phi \colon \Z/n\Z \to G, \qquad \bar\phi([k]_n) = g^k .
\]
It is well defined: if \([k]_n = [l]_n\) then \(n \mid (k - l)\), so
\(g^k = g^l\) by Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(2). It is a
homomorphism:
\(\bar\phi([k]_n + [l]_n) = \bar\phi([k+l]_n) = g^{k+l}
= \bar\phi([k]_n)\,\bar\phi([l]_n)\). It is surjective because \(\phi\)
is. And it is injective by
Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-kernel-injective}: if
\(\bar\phi([k]_n) = g^k = e\), then \(n \mid k\)
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(1)), so
\([k]_n = [0]_n\); the kernel of \(\bar\phi\) is trivial. Hence
\(\Z/n\Z \cong G\).

\emph{Distinctness.} Isomorphic groups have equal order, an isomorphism
being in particular a bijection. The group \(\Z\) is infinite and the
groups \(\Z/n\Z\) for \(n = 1, 2, 3, \dots\) have the pairwise distinct
finite orders \(n\), so no two of the listed groups are isomorphic, and
a cyclic group is isomorphic to exactly one of them---the one matching
its order. The final claim follows: cyclic groups of equal order are
isomorphic to the same listed group, hence to each other
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-iso-inverse}), and isomorphic groups have
equal order.
\end{proof}

The cheque presented at the head of the section can now be cashed. Fix
any finite group \(G\) and a public element \(g \in G\), and let
\(n = \ord(g)\). Associativity makes ``\(a\) repetitions of \(g\)''
unambiguous---the element \(g^a\), however bracketed, by generalised
associativity (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-genassoc})---and the
exponent laws give
\[
\bigl(g^a\bigr)^b = g^{ab} = \bigl(g^b\bigr)^a ,
\]
so the two parties of the opening protocol, each applying a secret
repetition count to the other's published value, really do arrive at a
common secret element. Repetition counts obey an arithmetic, not a mere
accumulation: by the classification theorem the subgroup
\(\langle g\rangle\) is a copy of \(\Z/n\Z\), so exponents add, negate,
and matter only modulo \(n\)
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides})---the modular arithmetic
of \cref{sec:intmod}, transplanted into the exponent. Lagrange's theorem
locates the modulus: \(n\) divides \(\abs{G}\), and
\(a^{\abs{G}} = e\) for every \(a \in G\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-aG-e}); in the unit groups
\((\Z/n\Z)^\times\) this specialises to Euler's congruence
\(a^{\varphi(n)} \equiv 1 \pmod{n}\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat}). Composition and inversion,
the first two demands of the opening problem, are thereby supplied in
full generality. The third demand---that publishing \(g^a\) should
\emph{hide} \(a\)---is not a theorem of algebra at all: it is a
statement about computation, and it fails in some groups (in
\((\Z/n\Z,+)\), recovering \(a\) from \(ag\) is a single modular
division) while appearing to hold in others. Making the demand precise,
and building the groups for which it is credible, occupies the sections
that follow and the volumes above them.

\section{Rings and fields}\label{sec:ringsfields}

An arithmetic circuit---the object a proof system ultimately asks a verifier
to check---is a network of gates, each of which either \emph{adds} or
\emph{multiplies} two previously computed values. A verifier must therefore
calculate in a number system where both operations make sense at once and
interact through the familiar laws of school algebra; and at certain
moments---inverting a random challenge, cancelling a common factor from both
sides of an identity---the verifier must \emph{divide} as well. In what kind
of structure can one do both, and when can one also divide? The structures of
genuine cryptographic interest all support two interacting operations obeying
the arithmetic laws of \(\Z\): the integers themselves, the rationals, the
residue classes modulo \(n\), polynomials, matrices. The abstract capture of
this pattern is the \emph{ring}; the key special case in which division by
every nonzero element is possible is the \emph{field}. Fields are the natural
arena for linear algebra and for the theory of polynomials, and the finite
fields \(\Fp\) and \(\Fq\) are the computational substrate of essentially all
the elliptic-curve and proof-system machinery later in the monograph.

The section also discharges a debt. Section~\ref{sec:intmod} verified,
concretely and one law at a time, that congruence classes admit an
arithmetic, and promised that the verified laws would receive their
structural names in due course. They receive them here: \(\Z/n\Z\) is
re-read as a ring, indeed as the quotient of the ring \(\Z\) by the ideal
\(n\Z\), and the theorem that \(\Z/p\Z\) is a field is stated in the
language that the rest of the series uses. As a standing convention we
assume from \S\ref{sec:groups} only the theory of \emph{abelian} groups;
additive structure is written additively with identity \(0\), multiplicative
structure multiplicatively with identity \(1\).

\subsection{Rings}\label{ringsfields:subsec-rings}

\begin{definition}[Ring]\label{ringsfields:def-ring}
A \emph{ring} is a set \(R\) equipped with two binary operations \(+\) and
\(\cdot\) such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \((R,+)\) is an abelian group: addition is associative and
  commutative, there is an element \(0\) with \(a+0=a\) for all \(a\), and
  every \(a\) has an \emph{additive inverse} \(-a\) with \(a+(-a)=0\);
\item multiplication is associative: \(a\cdot(b\cdot c)=(a\cdot b)\cdot c\);
\item there is a \emph{multiplicative identity} \(1\) with
  \(1\cdot a=a\cdot 1=a\) for all \(a\);
\item multiplication distributes over addition on both sides:
  \(a\cdot(b+c)=a\cdot b+a\cdot c\) and \((a+b)\cdot c=a\cdot c+b\cdot c\).
\end{enumerate}
The ring is \emph{commutative} if moreover \(a\cdot b=b\cdot a\) for all
\(a,b\in R\). We write \(ab\) for \(a\cdot b\), let multiplication bind
tighter than addition, and call \(0\) the \emph{zero} and \(1\) the
\emph{unity} of the ring.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{ringsfields:rem-conventions}
Conventions in the literature vary. Some authors omit requirement (3),
saying ``ring with unity'' for the notion above and ``rng'' for the weaker
one. In this monograph \emph{every ring has a \(1\)}, and the
structure-preserving maps between rings---the ring homomorphisms of
\cref{ringsfields:def-ringhom} below---are required to preserve it.
Moreover ``ring'' frequently means ``commutative ring'' in the examples
that matter to us; commutativity is always stated explicitly when a result
depends on it.
\end{remark}

The distributive law is the only axiom coupling the two operations, and a
surprising amount of everyday algebra follows from it alone. The following
identities are used constantly and, after this proposition, without comment.

\begin{proposition}[Elementary ring arithmetic]\label{ringsfields:prop-arith}
Let \(R\) be a ring and \(a,b\in R\). Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(0\cdot a=a\cdot 0=0\);
\item \((-a)b=a(-b)=-(ab)\);
\item \((-a)(-b)=ab\);
\item for all integers \(m,n\in\Z\), \((ma)(nb)=(mn)(ab)\), where \(na\)
  denotes the \(n\)-fold additive multiple (\(0a=0\), \((n+1)a=na+a\), and
  \((-n)a=-(na)\), as for any abelian group, \S\ref{sec:groups}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) From \(0=0+0\) and distributivity,
\(0\cdot a=(0+0)\cdot a=0\cdot a+0\cdot a\); adding \(-(0\cdot a)\) to both
sides leaves \(0=0\cdot a\). The computation for \(a\cdot 0\) is symmetric.

(2) By distributivity and (1),
\(ab+(-a)b=(a+(-a))b=0\cdot b=0\), so \((-a)b\) is the additive inverse of
\(ab\), that is, \((-a)b=-(ab)\); likewise \(ab+a(-b)=a(b+(-b))=0\) gives
\(a(-b)=-(ab)\).

(3) Applying (2) twice,
\((-a)(-b)=-\bigl(a(-b)\bigr)=-\bigl(-(ab)\bigr)=ab\), the last step because
negation is an involution in the abelian group \((R,+)\).

(4) First fix \(a,b\) and show \(a(nb)=n(ab)\) for \(n\ge 0\) by induction:
the case \(n=0\) is (1), and
\(a((n+1)b)=a(nb+b)=a(nb)+ab=n(ab)+ab=(n+1)(ab)\) by distributivity and the
inductive hypothesis. For \(n<0\) write \(n=-m\) with \(m>0\); then
\(a(nb)=a(-(mb))=-(a(mb))=-(m(ab))=n(ab)\) by (2). The same argument in the
first factor gives \((ma)c=m(ac)\) for all \(c\), and combining,
\[
(ma)(nb)=m\bigl(a(nb)\bigr)=m\bigl(n(ab)\bigr)=(mn)(ab),
\]
the final equality being the usual bookkeeping for iterated multiples in an
abelian group, which we omit.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The zero ring]\label{ringsfields:rem-zeroring}
Nothing in \cref{ringsfields:def-ring} forces \(1\neq 0\). If \(1=0\) in a
ring \(R\), then every \(a\in R\) satisfies
\(a=1\cdot a=0\cdot a=0\) by \cref{ringsfields:prop-arith}(1), so
\(R=\{0\}\): the \emph{zero ring} (or \emph{trivial ring}), a legitimate
ring in which both operations are the only possible ones. Whenever the zero
ring must be excluded we impose the assumption \(1\neq 0\) explicitly; the
definition of a field below builds \(1\neq 0\) in as a standing assumption.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Basic rings]\label{ringsfields:exa-rings}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The integers \(\Z\) form the prototype commutative ring; much of ring
  theory consists of isolating which features of \(\Z\) survive in
  generality.
\item The rationals \(\Q\), the reals \(\R\), and the complexes \(\C\) are
  commutative rings---indeed fields, in the sense of
  \S\ref{ringsfields:subsec-fields}.
\item The residue classes \(\Z/n\Z=\{\overline{0},\overline{1},\dots,
  \overline{n-1}\}\) for \(n\ge 1\)---in this section's examples we
  abbreviate the class \([a]_n\) of
  Definition~\ref{intmod:def:residueclass} to \(\overline{a}\) when the
  modulus is clear---under the induced operations form a
  commutative ring whose additive group is the cyclic group of order \(n\)
  (\S\ref{sec:groups}); this is \cref{ringsfields:thm-znring} below. We
  write \(\Z/n\Z\), or \(\Z_n\) when no confusion threatens. The case
  \(n=1\) gives the zero ring of \cref{ringsfields:rem-zeroring}.
\item The \emph{polynomial ring} \(R[x]\) over a commutative ring \(R\)
  consists of the polynomials \(a_0+a_1x+\dots+a_dx^d\) with coefficients
  \(a_i\in R\), added coefficient-wise and multiplied by expanding and
  collecting powers of \(x\); it is a commutative ring with the same \(0\)
  and \(1\) as \(R\) (the constant polynomials). The theory of polynomials
  over a field is developed in its own section later in the volume.
\item The \emph{matrix ring} \(\mathrm{M}_n(R)\) of \(n\times n\) matrices
  over a commutative ring \(R\), \(n\ge 1\), is a ring under matrix
  addition and multiplication. It is \emph{not} commutative for
  \(n\ge 2\), even over commutative \(R\): in \(\mathrm{M}_2(\Z)\),
  \[
  \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
  \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
  =\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad\text{but}\qquad
  \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
  \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
  =\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.
  \]
  This is the first important noncommutative example.
\item The \emph{product ring} \(R\times S\) of two rings carries the
  componentwise operations, with zero \((0,0)\) and identity \((1,1)\);
  likewise any finite product \(R_1\times\dots\times R_k\).
\item The zero ring \(\{0\}\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

The third example is the one this monograph computes in, and it is the one
whose ring structure was promised rather than named in \S\ref{sec:intmod}.
We now discharge that promise.

\begin{theorem}\label{ringsfields:thm-znring}
Let \(n\ge 1\). The operations
\[
[a]_n+[b]_n:=[a+b]_n, \qquad [a]_n\cdot[b]_n:=[ab]_n
\]
are well defined and make \(\Z/n\Z\) a commutative ring with additive
identity \([0]_n\) and multiplicative identity \([1]_n\). It has exactly
\(n\) elements.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The right-hand sides are defined in terms of chosen representatives \(a,b\),
so we must check that different choices give the same class. This is exactly
the compatibility half of Proposition~\ref{intmod:prop:congequiv}: if
\(a'\equiv a\) and \(b'\equiv b\pmod n\), then \(a'+b'\equiv a+b\) and
\(a'b'\equiv ab\pmod n\), so \([a'+b']_n=[a+b]_n\) and \([a'b']_n=[ab]_n\).
The operations are therefore well defined on classes.

Every ring axiom is now inherited from the corresponding law of \(\Z\) by
passing to classes. For instance, distributivity:
\begin{align*}
[a]_n\bigl([b]_n+[c]_n\bigr)
&=[a]_n[b+c]_n=[a(b+c)]_n=[ab+ac]_n\\
&=[ab]_n+[ac]_n=[a]_n[b]_n+[a]_n[c]_n,
\end{align*}
using only the definitions and the distributive law of the integers.
Associativity and commutativity of both operations follow by the identical
pattern. The class \([0]_n\) is an additive identity and \([1]_n\) a
multiplicative one, since \([a+0]_n=[a]_n\) and \([1\cdot a]_n=[a]_n\); and
\(-[a]_n=[-a]_n\) because \([a]_n+[-a]_n=[a+(-a)]_n=[0]_n\).

Finally, by the division algorithm every integer is congruent modulo \(n\)
to exactly one of \(0,1,\dots,n-1\) (the least nonnegative residues,
Definition~\ref{intmod:def:residueclass} and the discussion following it),
so the classes \([0]_n,\dots,[n-1]_n\) are distinct and exhaust \(\Z/n\Z\):
the ring has exactly \(n\) elements.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Units, zero divisors, and integral
domains}\label{ringsfields:subsec-unitsdomains}

Two features of the integers now deserve names of their own. First, \(\Z\)
enjoys \emph{cancellation}: a product of nonzero integers is nonzero, so a
nonzero common factor may be struck from both sides of an equation. Second,
division \emph{within} \(\Z\) is nonetheless rare: only \(\pm 1\) divide
\(1\). The next two definitions abstract these phenomena---units capture the
elements one can divide by, zero divisors the obstruction to cancelling.

\begin{definition}[Unit]\label{ringsfields:def-unit}
An element \(u\) of a ring \(R\) is a \emph{unit} (or is \emph{invertible})
if there exists \(v\in R\) with \(uv=vu=1\). Such a \(v\) is unique: if
\(uv=vu=1\) and \(uv'=v'u=1\), then
\(v=v\cdot 1=v(uv')=(vu)v'=1\cdot v'=v'\). It is called the \emph{inverse}
of \(u\) and written \(u^{-1}\). The set of units of \(R\) is denoted
\(R^\times\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{ringsfields:prop-units-group}
For any ring \(R\), the set \(R^\times\) of units is a group under the
multiplication of \(R\), called the \emph{group of units} (or
\emph{multiplicative group}) of \(R\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The identity \(1\) is a unit (it is its own inverse), so \(1\in R^\times\).
For closure, let \(u,w\in R^\times\); then
\[
(uw)(w^{-1}u^{-1})=u(ww^{-1})u^{-1}=u\cdot 1\cdot u^{-1}=1,
\]
and symmetrically \((w^{-1}u^{-1})(uw)=1\), so \(uw\) is a unit with
\((uw)^{-1}=w^{-1}u^{-1}\). Multiplication in \(R^\times\) is associative
because it is associative in \(R\). Finally each \(u^{-1}\) is itself a
unit, its inverse being \(u\); so every element of \(R^\times\) has an
inverse in \(R^\times\), and the group axioms are verified.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Units]\label{ringsfields:exa-units}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The units of the integers are \(\Z^\times=\{1,-1\}\): if \(uv=1\)
  in \(\Z\) then \(|u|\,|v|=1\) forces \(|u|=1\).
\item In \(\Q\), \(\R\), and \(\C\) every nonzero element is a unit:
  \(\Q^\times=\Q\setminus\{0\}\), \(\R^\times=\R\setminus\{0\}\),
  \(\C^\times=\C\setminus\{0\}\). Commutative rings with \(1\neq 0\) in
  which \emph{every} nonzero element is a unit are precisely the fields
  of \S\ref{ringsfields:subsec-fields}; this is their defining feature.
  Both qualifiers are needed: the zero ring of
  \cref{ringsfields:rem-zeroring} satisfies the unit condition
  vacuously, and noncommutative rings satisfying it---the
  \emph{division rings}---exist but are not fields.
\item In \(\Z/n\Z\), the class \(\overline{a}\) is a unit if and only if
  \(\gcd(a,n)=1\), by Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff}; thus
  \((\Z/n\Z)^\times\) consists of the residue classes coprime to the
  modulus, and its order is Euler's totient \(\varphi(n)\)
  (Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}).
\item In the matrix ring, \(\mathrm{M}_n(R)^\times\) is the group of
  invertible \(n\times n\) matrices. Over a field it is the \emph{general
  linear group} \(\mathrm{GL}_n\), the matrices of nonzero determinant;
  the determinant belongs to linear algebra, treated later in the volume.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Zero divisor]\label{ringsfields:def-zerodiv}
A \emph{nonzero} element \(a\) of a ring \(R\) is a \emph{left zero
divisor} if \(ab=0\) for some nonzero \(b\in R\), and a \emph{right zero
divisor} if \(ba=0\) for some nonzero \(b\). In a commutative ring the two
notions coincide and we say simply \emph{zero divisor}. A nonzero element
that is neither is called \emph{regular} (or \emph{cancellable}).
\end{definition}

The terminology ``cancellable'' is justified by the following equivalence:
zero divisors are exactly the obstruction to dividing out a common factor.

\begin{proposition}\label{ringsfields:prop-cancel}
A nonzero element \(a\) of a ring \(R\) is not a left zero divisor if and
only if it is \emph{left-cancellable}: \(ab=ac\) implies \(b=c\) for all
\(b,c\in R\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose \(a\) is not a left zero divisor and \(ab=ac\). Then
\(a(b-c)=ab-ac=0\), using \cref{ringsfields:prop-arith}(2) to expand
\(a(b+(-c))\); since \(a\) is nonzero and not a left zero divisor, the
factor \(b-c\) must be \(0\), so \(b=c\). Conversely, suppose \(a\) is a
left zero divisor, say \(ab=0\) with \(b\neq 0\). Then \(ab=0=a\cdot 0\)
while \(b\neq 0\): cancellation fails.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{ringsfields:rem-unit-not-zd}
A unit is never a zero divisor: if \(u\in R^\times\) and \(ub=0\), then
\(b=1\cdot b=(u^{-1}u)b=u^{-1}(ub)=u^{-1}\cdot 0=0\). The converse fails in
general---in \(\Z\) every nonzero element is a non-zero-divisor, yet only
\(\pm 1\) are units---but it \emph{does} hold in finite commutative rings,
by the injectivity-implies-surjectivity argument that proves
\cref{ringsfields:thm-finite-domain} below.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Zero divisors]\label{ringsfields:exa-zerodiv}
\begin{enumerate}
\item In \(\Z/6\Z\),
  \(\overline{2}\cdot\overline{3}=\overline{6}=\overline{0}\), so
  \(\overline{2}\) and \(\overline{3}\) are zero divisors. In general a
  nonzero class \(\overline{a}\in\Z/n\Z\) is a zero divisor precisely when
  \(d:=\gcd(a,n)>1\): in that case
  \(a\cdot(n/d)=(a/d)\cdot n\equiv 0\pmod n\) while
  \(\overline{n/d}\neq\overline{0}\) (as \(0<n/d<n\)); and when \(d=1\) the
  class is a unit (Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-units}(3)), hence no zero
  divisor by \cref{ringsfields:rem-unit-not-zd}. Thus in \(\Z/n\Z\) every
  nonzero element is either a unit or a zero divisor---a dichotomy revisited
  from a structural angle by \cref{ringsfields:thm-finite-domain}.
\item In a product ring \(R\times S\) with both factors nonzero,
  \((1,0)(0,1)=(0,0)\): product rings always have zero divisors.
\item In \(\mathrm{M}_2(\R)\), the diagonal matrices
  \(\operatorname{diag}(1,0)\) and \(\operatorname{diag}(0,1)\) multiply to
  the zero matrix; more generally every nonzero \emph{singular}
  matrix---the standard name for a square matrix that is not
  invertible---is a zero divisor there.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Integral domain]\label{ringsfields:def-domain}
An \emph{integral domain} (or simply \emph{domain}) is a commutative ring
\(R\) with \(1\neq 0\) and no zero divisors: \(ab=0\) implies \(a=0\) or
\(b=0\). Equivalently, by \cref{ringsfields:prop-cancel}, a domain is a
nonzero commutative ring in which every nonzero element is cancellable.
\end{definition}

The residue rings sort themselves neatly against this definition, and the
sorting is governed by primality.

\begin{proposition}\label{ringsfields:prop-zndomain}
Let \(n\ge 2\). The ring \(\Z/n\Z\) has no zero divisors if and only if
\(n\) is prime; that is, \(\Z/n\Z\) is an integral domain if and only if
\(n\) is prime.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If \(n=ab\) is composite with \(1<a,b<n\), then
\([a]_n[b]_n=[n]_n=[0]_n\) with both factors nonzero (their representatives
lie strictly between \(0\) and \(n\)), so \(\Z/n\Z\) has zero divisors.
Conversely let \(n=p\) be prime and suppose \([a]_p[b]_p=[0]_p\), that is,
\(p\mid ab\). Euclid's lemma for primes
(Lemma~\ref{intmod:lem:primedivides}) gives \(p\mid a\) or \(p\mid b\),
that is, \([a]_p=[0]_p\) or \([b]_p=[0]_p\). Since \(p\ge 2\) we also have
\([1]_p\neq[0]_p\), so \(\Z/p\Z\) is an integral domain.
\end{proof}

For prime \(p\) the ring \(\Z/p\Z\) is in fact far better than a domain:
every nonzero class is invertible, so it is a \emph{field}---a term defined
in \S\ref{ringsfields:subsec-fields}, where the statement is proved as
\cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}.

\begin{example}[Integral domains]\label{ringsfields:exa-domain}
The integers \(\Z\) form an integral domain---the example the name
commemorates. Every field is a domain
(\cref{ringsfields:prop-field-domain} below). The ring \(\Z/n\Z\) is a
domain exactly when \(n\) is prime
(\cref{ringsfields:prop-zndomain}; the composite case is witnessed
concretely by Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-zerodiv}(1)). If \(R\) is a
domain, so is the polynomial ring \(R[x]\): let \(f\) and \(g\) be
nonzero of degrees \(m\) and \(n\)---the largest indices carrying
nonzero coefficients \(f_m\) and \(g_n\), the \emph{leading
coefficients}. In \(fg\) the coefficient of \(x^{m+n}\) is
\(\sum_{i+j=m+n} f_i g_j\); every term with \(i>m\) or \(j>n\) has a
vanishing factor, and \(i\le m\), \(j\le n\), \(i+j=m+n\) force
\(i=m\), \(j=n\), so the coefficient equals \(f_m g_n\), nonzero
because \(R\) is a domain. Hence \(fg\neq 0\). Non-domains include
\(\Z/6\Z\), all product
rings, and the matrix rings \(\mathrm{M}_n(R)\) for \(n\ge 2\)
(Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-zerodiv}).
\end{example}

\subsection{Ideals and quotient rings}\label{ringsfields:subsec-ideals}

We next carry quotient formation from groups to rings. For an abelian group
and any subgroup, the cosets themselves form a group
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-quotient-abelian}); the ring-theoretic
analogue asks for the subsets of a ring by which one can quotient while
keeping \emph{both} operations, and the answer is the notion of an
\emph{ideal}. The treatment here is deliberately brief: we need ideals
chiefly to construct \(\Z/n\Z\) conceptually and to indicate where the
finite fields \(\Fq\) come from; a fuller development belongs to
commutative algebra.

\begin{definition}[Ideal]\label{ringsfields:def-ideal}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring. An \emph{ideal} of \(R\) is a subset
\(I\subseteq R\) such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(I\) is a subgroup of the additive group \((R,+)\): \(0\in I\), and
  \(a-b\in I\) whenever \(a,b\in I\); and
\item \(I\) \emph{absorbs} multiplication by arbitrary ring elements:
  \(ra\in I\) whenever \(r\in R\) and \(a\in I\).
\end{enumerate}
In noncommutative rings one distinguishes left, right, and two-sided
ideals according to the side on which absorption holds; commutative rings
suffice for this volume, and there the three notions coincide.
\end{definition}

Despite containing \(0\) and being closed under the ring's operations in
the sense above, an ideal is almost never a subring: if an ideal \(I\)
contains \(1\)---or, by the same absorption argument, any unit \(u\), since
then \(1=u^{-1}u\in I\)---then \(r=r\cdot 1\in I\) for every \(r\in R\),
so \(I=R\). The only ideal containing a unit is the whole ring. This little
observation will carry real weight: it is the reason a field has no ideals
other than the two trivial ones, and hence no interesting quotients.

\begin{example}[Ideals]\label{ringsfields:exa-ideal}
\begin{enumerate}
\item In any commutative ring \(R\), the subsets \(\{0\}\) and \(R\) are
  ideals---the \emph{trivial} and \emph{improper} ideals respectively.
\item For \(a\in R\), the \emph{principal ideal generated by \(a\)} is
  \[
  (a):=aR=\{\,ar:r\in R\,\},
  \]
  the set of multiples of \(a\); absorption holds because
  \(r'(ar)=a(r'r)\). In \(\Z\), the principal ideal \((n)=n\Z\) is the set
  of multiples of \(n\). In fact \emph{every} ideal of \(\Z\) is principal:
  an ideal \(I\neq\{0\}\) contains a nonzero element and hence (closing
  under negation) a positive one, so it contains a least positive element
  \(n\) by well-ordering; for any \(a\in I\) the division algorithm gives
  \(a=qn+r\) with \(0\le r<n\), whence \(r=a-qn\in I\) by absorption and
  subgroup closure, forcing \(r=0\) by minimality; so \(I=n\Z\). This
  sharpens the observation of Remark~\ref{intmod:rem:strongestgcd}, where
  ideals of \(\Z\) first appeared as the sets of B\'ezout combinations.
\item For \(a_1,\dots,a_k\in R\), the \emph{ideal generated by}
  \(a_1,\dots,a_k\) is
  \((a_1,\dots,a_k)=\{\,r_1a_1+\dots+r_ka_k:r_i\in R\,\}\), the smallest
  ideal containing them all.
\end{enumerate}
An integral domain in which every ideal is principal, as in (2), is called
a \emph{principal ideal domain} (PID).
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Quotient ring operations]\label{ringsfields:def-quotient}
Let \(I\) be an ideal of a commutative ring \(R\). Since \(I\) is a
subgroup of the abelian group \((R,+)\), the additive cosets \(a+I\) form
the quotient group \(R/I\) under coset addition
\((a+I)+(b+I)=(a+b)+I\) (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-quotient-abelian}).
Define a multiplication of cosets by
\[
(a+I)(b+I):=ab+I.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{ringsfields:prop-quotient-well-defined}
With the operations of \cref{ringsfields:def-quotient}, the set \(R/I\) is
a commutative ring---the \emph{quotient ring} of \(R\) by \(I\)---with zero
\(0+I=I\) and identity \(1+I\). In particular the coset multiplication is
well defined.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Well-definedness is the point at which absorption is needed. Let
\(a'=a+s\) and \(b'=b+t\) be other representatives of the same cosets,
with \(s,t\in I\). Then
\[
a'b'=(a+s)(b+t)=ab+at+sb+st,
\]
and each of \(at\), \(sb\), \(st\) lies in \(I\) by the absorption
property; hence \(a'b'-ab\in I\) and \(a'b'+I=ab+I\). The product coset is
therefore independent of the representatives chosen.

The ring axioms now pass to cosets exactly as in the proof of
\cref{ringsfields:thm-znring}. For instance, distributivity reads
\begin{align*}
(a+I)\bigl((b+I)+(c+I)\bigr)
&=(a+I)\bigl((b+c)+I\bigr)=a(b+c)+I\\
&=(ab+ac)+I=(a+I)(b+I)+(a+I)(c+I),
\end{align*}
and associativity and commutativity of both operations follow by the same
pattern from the corresponding laws in \(R\). The coset \(I=0+I\) is the
additive identity, \(1+I\) the multiplicative identity, and
\(-(a+I)=(-a)+I\).
\end{proof}

One more definition names the maps under which ring structure is preserved;
we need it to say precisely in what sense the quotient construction
generalises the passage from \(\Z\) to \(\Z/n\Z\).

\begin{definition}[Ring homomorphism]\label{ringsfields:def-ringhom}
A \emph{ring homomorphism} between rings \(R\) and \(S\) is a map
\(\phi\colon R\to S\) with
\[
\phi(a+b)=\phi(a)+\phi(b), \qquad \phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b), \qquad
\phi(1)=1
\]
for all \(a,b\in R\). Its \emph{kernel} is
\(\ker\phi=\{\,a\in R:\phi(a)=0\,\}\).
\end{definition}

The kernel of a ring homomorphism out of a commutative ring is always an
ideal: it is a subgroup of \((R,+)\) because \(\phi\) is in particular a
homomorphism of additive groups, and it absorbs because \(a\in\ker\phi\)
gives \(\phi(ra)=\phi(r)\phi(a)=\phi(r)\cdot 0=0\).

\begin{example}\label{ringsfields:exa-quotient-Zn}
Take \(R=\Z\) and \(I=n\Z\). The cosets \(a+n\Z\) are literally the residue
classes \([a]_n\) of Definition~\ref{intmod:def:residueclass}, and the
coset operations of \cref{ringsfields:def-quotient} are exactly residue
arithmetic: the quotient construction recovers the ring \(\Z/n\Z\) of
Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-rings}(3), and is the conceptual origin of
modular arithmetic. The same construction with a different ring produces
the general finite fields: quotients \(\Fp[x]/(f)\) of a polynomial ring by
the principal ideal of an \emph{irreducible} polynomial \(f\) of degree
\(k\)---irreducibility, the polynomial analogue of primality, is defined
in \S\ref{sec:polynomials}---furnish the finite fields \(\Fq\) with
\(q=p^k\) elements. We record the recipe here and take it up in
\S\ref{sec:finitefields}; the curves of Halo~2 and Orchard work over
\emph{prime} fields, so the prime case \(\Fp=\Z/p\Z\) carries the main
line of development.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{ringsfields:rem-quotientring}
Structurally, then, \(\Z/n\Z\) is the quotient of the ring \(\Z\) by the
ideal \(n\Z=\{\,kn:k\in\Z\,\}\), and the map
\[
\pi\colon\Z\to\Z/n\Z, \qquad \pi(a)=[a]_n,
\]
is a surjective ring homomorphism with kernel \(n\Z\). This is the
prototype of every quotient construction used in the volume's algebra: one
starts from a ring, singles out an ideal of elements to be ``declared
zero'', and computes with cosets. On notation: we abbreviate \([a]_n\)
to \(\overline{a}\) (as in the examples above) or drop the decoration
altogether, writing plain \(a\), whenever the modulus is clear from
context; \(\Z/n\Z\) and \(\Z_n\) are used interchangeably; and \(\Z_n\)
always means this ring, never the \(n\)-adic integers (which do not appear
in this series).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Fields}\label{ringsfields:subsec-fields}

\begin{definition}[Field]\label{ringsfields:def-field}
A \emph{field} is a commutative ring \(F\) with \(1\neq 0\) in which every
nonzero element is a unit. Equivalently, a field is a set \(F\) with two
operations \(+\) and \(\cdot\) such that \((F,+)\) is an abelian group with
identity \(0\), the nonzero elements \((F\setminus\{0\},\cdot)\) form an
abelian group with identity \(1\), and multiplication distributes over
addition. Unwinding the definition: in a field one may add, subtract,
multiply, and divide by any nonzero element, with \(a/b:=ab^{-1}\). The
group \(F^\times=(F\setminus\{0\},\cdot)\) is the \emph{multiplicative
group} of the field. (Two points of the equivalence are not direct
transcriptions. Passing from the first formulation to the second, one
must show \(F\setminus\{0\}\) is closed under multiplication; that is
\cref{ringsfields:prop-field-domain} below. Passing back, the ring
axioms must be checked for products involving \(0\): distributivity
forces \(0\cdot a=a\cdot 0=0\) by the computation of
\cref{ringsfields:prop-arith}(1), whereupon every product with a zero
factor vanishes and the associativity, commutativity, and identity
laws extend from \(F\setminus\{0\}\) to all of \(F\); and \(1\neq 0\)
holds because the group \(F\setminus\{0\}\) contains its identity
\(1\).)
\end{definition}

The finite world of \S\ref{sec:intmod} already contains the series' most
important fields, and the unit criterion proved there identifies them at
once.

\begin{corollary}\label{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}
If \(p\) is prime, then every nonzero class in \(\Z/p\Z\) is a unit, so
\(\Z/p\Z\) is a field; it is denoted \(\Fp\). More generally, \(\Z/n\Z\) is
a field if and only if \(n\) is prime.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let \(p\) be prime and \([a]_p\neq[0]_p\), so that \(p\nmid a\). The only
positive divisors of \(p\) are \(1\) and \(p\), so \(\gcd(a,p)=1\), and
Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff} makes \([a]_p\) a unit. Since
\([1]_p\neq[0]_p\) for \(p\ge 2\), the ring \(\Z/p\Z\) is a field.
Conversely, if \(n=ab\) is composite with \(1<a,b<n\), then
\([a]_n\neq[0]_n\) but \(\gcd(a,n)=a>1\), so \([a]_n\) is not a unit
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff} again) and the ring is not a field; and
\(n=1\) gives the zero ring, which is not a field because a field has
\(1\neq 0\) by definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Fields]\label{ringsfields:exa-fields}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The rationals \(\Q\), the reals \(\R\), and the complexes \(\C\)
  are fields. The integers are not: \(2\) has no inverse in \(\Z\).
\item For \(p\) prime, \(\Fp:=\Z/p\Z\) is a field
  (\cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}; \cref{ringsfields:thm-finite-domain}
  below recovers the same fact abstractly). It is called the \emph{prime
  field} of characteristic \(p\)---\cref{ringsfields:def-char} names the
  invariant---and is fundamental to every later cryptographic
  construction in the series. Its finite extensions \(\Fq\) with
  \(q=p^k\) elements exist for every prime power, arising from the
  quotient recipe of Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-quotient-Zn}, and are
  developed in \S\ref{sec:finitefields}.
\item The subset \(\Q(i)=\{\,a+bi:a,b\in\Q\,\}\subseteq\C\) is a field:
  the inverse of a nonzero \(a+bi\) is \((a-bi)/(a^2+b^2)\), which again
  has rational coordinates. More generally the \emph{number fields}, obtained by
  adjoining to \(\Q\) roots of polynomial equations with rational
  coefficients, are fields.
\item For any field \(F\), the \emph{rational functions}
  \(F(x)=\{\,f/g:f,g\in F[x],\ g\neq 0\,\}\) form a field under the usual
  arithmetic of fractions---the field of fractions of the polynomial ring
  \(F[x]\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{ringsfields:prop-field-domain}
Every field is an integral domain.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(F\) be a field. Commutativity and \(1\neq 0\) hold by definition.
Suppose \(ab=0\) with \(a\neq 0\). Then \(a\) is a unit, and multiplying
by its inverse,
\[
b=1\cdot b=(a^{-1}a)b=a^{-1}(ab)=a^{-1}\cdot 0=0.
\]
Hence \(F\) has no zero divisors.
\end{proof}

The converse fails: \(\Z\) is a domain but not a field
(Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-domain}). Finiteness,
however, closes the gap entirely, and does so by a counting device worth
naming, for it recurs. Call it the \emph{injectivity-implies-surjectivity
argument}: to invert an element \(a\) of a finite structure, show that
multiplication by \(a\) is injective, conclude that it is surjective
because an injective map from a finite set to itself must be onto, and
read off a preimage of \(1\). The same device proves the claim of
\cref{ringsfields:rem-unit-not-zd} that in a finite commutative ring every
non-zero-divisor is a unit.

\begin{theorem}\label{ringsfields:thm-finite-domain}
Every finite integral domain is a field.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let \(R\) be a finite integral domain and \(a\in R\) nonzero. Consider the
multiplication map
\[
\mu_a\colon R\to R, \qquad \mu_a(x)=ax.
\]
It is injective: if \(ax=ay\), then \(a(x-y)=0\), and since \(R\) is a
domain and \(a\neq 0\) this forces \(x-y=0\), that is, \(x=y\)
(equivalently, \(a\) is cancellable by \cref{ringsfields:prop-cancel}). An
injective map from a finite set to itself is surjective: the image
\(\mu_a(R)\) has \(|R|\) distinct elements and sits inside \(R\), so it is
all of \(R\). In particular \(1\) lies in the image, so \(ab=1\) for some
\(b\in R\); commutativity gives \(ba=1\) as well, so \(b=a^{-1}\) and \(a\)
is a unit. Since \(R\) is a domain, \(1\neq 0\), and every nonzero element
has just been shown invertible: \(R\) is a field.
\end{proof}

Applied to \(\Z/p\Z\)---a finite domain by
\cref{ringsfields:prop-zndomain}---the theorem recovers
\cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}. The two proofs differ instructively. The
pigeonhole argument merely asserts that each inverse \emph{exists}; the
extended Euclidean algorithm of \S\ref{sec:intmod} \emph{computes} it, in
\(O(\log p)\) division steps. Cryptography needs both: the abstract
statement to reason with, and the algorithm to run.

\subsection{The characteristic}\label{ringsfields:subsec-char}

Every ring receives a canonical map from the integers, and the behaviour of
that map is a fundamental invariant: it measures how much of \(\Z\)
survives inside the ring.

\begin{definition}[Characteristic]\label{ringsfields:def-char}
Let \(R\) be a ring, and for \(n\in\Z\) let \(n\cdot 1\) denote the
\(n\)-fold additive multiple of \(1\) (so \(n\cdot 1 = 1+\dots+1\) with
\(n\) summands for \(n>0\), \(0\cdot 1=0\), and
\((-n)\cdot 1=-(n\cdot 1)\)). The \emph{characteristic} \(\charf(R)\) is
the least positive integer \(n\) with \(n\cdot 1=0\), if such an \(n\)
exists, and \(0\) otherwise.
\end{definition}

An equivalent formulation is often more useful. The map
\[
\iota\colon\Z\to R, \qquad \iota(n)=n\cdot 1,
\]
is a ring homomorphism: additivity is the bookkeeping of iterated
multiples in the abelian group \((R,+)\), multiplicativity is
\cref{ringsfields:prop-arith}(4) with \(a=b=1\)---namely
\(\iota(m)\iota(n)=(m\cdot 1)(n\cdot 1)=(mn)(1\cdot 1)=\iota(mn)\)---and
\(\iota(1)=1\). Its kernel is an ideal of \(\Z\), hence of the form
\(n\Z\) for a unique \(n\ge 0\) by Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-ideal}(2),
and \(\charf(R)\) is exactly this nonnegative generator: \(\charf(R)=0\)
precisely when \(\iota\) is injective, so that a faithful copy of \(\Z\)
sits inside \(R\), and \(\charf(R)=n>0\) when the kernel is \(n\Z\).

\begin{example}[Characteristics]\label{ringsfields:exa-char}
The rings \(\Z\), \(\Q\), \(\R\), and \(\C\) all have characteristic \(0\):
no positive multiple of \(1\) vanishes. For the residue rings,
\(\charf(\Z/n\Z)=n\): the multiple \(n\cdot\overline{1}=\overline{n}
=\overline{0}\) vanishes, and no smaller positive multiple does, since
\(k\cdot\overline{1}=\overline{k}\neq\overline{0}\) for \(0<k<n\). In
particular \(\charf(\Fp)=p\).
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}\label{ringsfields:thm-char-prime}
The characteristic of an integral domain---in particular, of a field---is
either \(0\) or a prime number.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let \(R\) be an integral domain with \(\charf(R)=n>0\); we show \(n\) is
prime. First \(n\neq 1\): otherwise \(1\cdot 1=1=0\), making \(R\) the
zero ring (\cref{ringsfields:rem-zeroring}) and contradicting \(1\neq 0\)
in a domain. Suppose \(n=ab\) with \(1<a,b<n\). By
\cref{ringsfields:prop-arith}(4),
\[
0=n\cdot 1=(ab)(1\cdot 1)=(a\cdot 1)(b\cdot 1),
\]
so \(a\cdot 1=0\) or \(b\cdot 1=0\) since \(R\) is a domain. Either case
exhibits a positive integer smaller than \(n\) whose multiple of \(1\)
vanishes, contradicting the minimality of \(n=\charf(R)\). Hence \(n\)
admits no such factorisation, and being greater than \(1\), it is prime.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The prime subfield]\label{ringsfields:rem-prime-subfield}
Let \(F\) be a field. If \(\charf(F)=0\), the homomorphism \(\iota\)
embeds a copy of \(\Z\) in \(F\), and dividing---possible in a field---a
copy of \(\Q\) as well. If \(\charf(F)=p\), the image of \(\iota\) is a
copy of \(\Fp=\Z/p\Z\). The smallest subfield so obtained is called the
\emph{prime subfield} of \(F\); every field is thus an extension of \(\Q\)
or of some \(\Fp\), and the dichotomy between characteristic zero and
characteristic \(p\) pervades the subject. The fields \(\Fq\) underlying
the cryptography treated in this series all have prime characteristic
\(p\). There the ``freshman's dream'' identity \((a+b)^p=a^p+b^p\)
actually holds, and gives rise to the Frobenius endomorphism; both are
developed in the finite-fields section
(\cref{finitefields:lem:freshman}).
\end{remark}

The question that opened the section is now answered in full. A verifier
that must add and multiply computes in a ring; the laws verified
one at a time for \(\Z/n\Z\) in \S\ref{sec:intmod} are precisely the ring
axioms, and the construction that produced them is the quotient of \(\Z\)
by the ideal \(n\Z\). A verifier that must also divide computes in a
field, and \cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield} says exactly which moduli
provide one: the primes. The payoff theorem,
\cref{ringsfields:thm-finite-domain}, closes the circle with a purely
structural guarantee: in a finite world there is no daylight between the
absence of zero divisors and the presence of division---any nonzero
finite commutative ring in which nonzero elements never multiply to zero
is automatically a field. The prime fields \(\Fp\) certified by these
results are the ground on which the volume now builds: polynomials,
linear algebra, roots of unity, and elliptic curves are all developed
over them in the sections that follow.

\section{Polynomials over a field}\label{sec:polynomials}

Throughout this section \(F\) denotes a field. Consider a problem that
sounds impossible. Two parties each hold a polynomial of enormous
degree---millions of coefficients, say, the encoding of an entire
computation---and wish to convince themselves that the two polynomials are
equal. Comparing coefficient lists costs as much as reading the
computation itself. Could a \emph{single} spot check suffice: pick one
value \(\alpha\) at random, evaluate both polynomials there, and declare
them equal if the two field elements agree? The astonishing answer is
yes---with an error probability so small that no adversary can exploit
it---and this section develops exactly the algebra that makes the answer
honest.

Polynomials are the engine behind finite fields, error-correcting codes,
and---most importantly here---the polynomial commitment schemes and
\emph{arithmetisation} (the encoding of a computation as identities
between polynomials) underlying Halo~2 and Zcash Orchard. The single most
exploited fact in that edifice is elementary: a nonzero polynomial of
degree \(d\) over a field has at most \(d\) roots. From this one fact the
section extracts Lagrange interpolation and the Schwartz--Zippel
principle, the probabilistic heart of every interactive proof we
eventually construct. The only prerequisite is the field theory of the
preceding section.

\subsection{The polynomial ring}\label{polynomials:sub:ring}

One point of principle governs everything that follows, so we state it
before the first definition: \emph{a polynomial is not a function}. It is
a formal expression---at bottom, a finite list of coefficients---which one
may \emph{evaluate} to obtain a function, but which in general carries
more information than that function. Over the finite fields of
cryptographic interest the distinction is not pedantic but essential, and
the closing subsections of this section
(\S\ref{polynomials:sub:formalfn}) return to it at length.

\begin{definition}[Polynomial]\label{polynomials:def:polynomial}
A \emph{polynomial} over \(F\) in the \emph{indeterminate} \(X\) is a
formal expression
\[
f \;=\; a_0 + a_1 X + a_2 X^2 + \dots + a_n X^n \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i X^i,
\]
where \(n \in \N = \{0,1,2,\dots\}\) and the \emph{coefficients}
\(a_0,\dots,a_n\) lie in \(F\). Formally, \(f\) is identified with its
coefficient sequence \((a_0,a_1,a_2,\dots)\), in which only finitely many
entries are nonzero. Two polynomials are equal precisely when their
coefficient sequences agree term by term:
\[
\sum_i a_i X^i = \sum_i b_i X^i
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
a_i = b_i \text{ for every } i \in \N .
\]
The symbol \(X\) is an \emph{indeterminate} (a formal variable): it is not
an element of \(F\) and stands for nothing in particular; it is merely a
placeholder organising the coefficients. The set of all polynomials over
\(F\) in \(X\) is denoted \(F[X]\).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The sequence model]\label{polynomials:rem:sequence-model}
The fully rigorous rendering of \cref{polynomials:def:polynomial} takes
\(F[X]\) to be the set of all functions \(a\colon \N \to F\) that are
\emph{eventually zero}: there is some \(N\) with \(a(i) = 0\) for all
\(i > N\). The expression \(\sum_i a_i X^i\) is then merely suggestive
notation for the sequence \((a_0,a_1,\dots)\). We use the two viewpoints
interchangeably; the notation is justified because the arithmetic of these
sequences, defined next, is exactly the arithmetic the notation suggests.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[{Ring operations on \(F[X]\)}]
\label{polynomials:def:operations}
Let \(f = \sum_i a_i X^i\) and \(g = \sum_i b_i X^i\) lie in \(F[X]\),
with the shorter coefficient sequence zero-padded so that both run over
the same index set. Their \emph{sum} and \emph{product} are
\[
f + g \;=\; \sum_{i \ge 0} (a_i + b_i)\, X^i,
\qquad
f g \;=\; \sum_{k \ge 0} c_k X^k
\quad\text{where}\quad
c_k \;=\; \sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{k} a_i b_{k-i} .
\]
The coefficient \(c_k\) is the \emph{convolution} of the two coefficient
sequences. Both defining sums are finite---only finitely many \(a_i\) and
\(b_j\) are nonzero, hence only finitely many \(c_k\) are nonzero---so the
sum and the product are again polynomials.
\end{definition}

The convolution formula is exactly what multiplying out
\((\sum_i a_i X^i)(\sum_j b_j X^j)\) and collecting like powers of \(X\)
gives, using \(X^i \cdot X^j = X^{i+j}\). Stated as a convolution,
however, it makes no appeal to substituting a value for \(X\): it is a
purely formal manipulation of coefficient sequences. The formal/functional
distinction announced above is thereby kept intact at the level of the
operations themselves.

The verification that these operations obey the ring axioms
(\cref{ringsfields:def-ring}) is a single device applied over and over:
to prove an identity between polynomials, compute the coefficient of
\(X^m\) on each side as a finite sum over index decompositions, then
reindex or regroup that sum. No evaluation is ever involved. The
associativity step below is the archetype.

\begin{theorem}[{\(F[X]\) is a commutative ring}]\label{polynomials:thm:ring}
With the operations of \cref{polynomials:def:operations}, the set
\(F[X]\) is a commutative ring whose additive identity is the zero
polynomial \(0 = (0,0,0,\dots)\) and whose multiplicative identity is the
constant polynomial \(1 = (1,0,0,\dots)\). Moreover the map
\(c \mapsto (c,0,0,\dots)\) embeds \(F\) into \(F[X]\) as a subring, the
\emph{constant polynomials}; in this sense \(F \subseteq F[X]\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Additive structure.} Addition is coordinatewise, so its
commutativity and associativity are inherited directly from the
corresponding laws of the additive group of \(F\), the sequence
\((0,0,\dots)\) is an additive identity, and
\((-a_0,-a_1,\dots)\) is an additive inverse of \((a_0,a_1,\dots)\).
Hence \((F[X],+)\) is an abelian group.

\emph{Commutativity of multiplication.} The coefficient of \(X^k\) in
\(fg\) is \(\sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j\); reindexing the sum by
\((i,j) \mapsto (j,i)\) and using commutativity of multiplication in
\(F\),
\[
\sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j \;=\; \sum_{i+j=k} b_i a_j,
\]
which is the coefficient of \(X^k\) in \(gf\).

\emph{Associativity of multiplication.} Write \(h = \sum_l c_l X^l\).
The coefficient of \(X^m\) in \((fg)h\) is
\[
\sum_{k+l=m} \Bigl(\sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j\Bigr) c_l
\;=\; \sum_{i+j+l=m} a_i b_j c_l ,
\]
and expanding \(f(gh)\) instead produces
\(\sum_{i+j+l=m} a_i (b_j c_l)\), the same symmetric triple sum by
associativity in \(F\). Hence \((fg)h = f(gh)\) coefficient by
coefficient.

\emph{Distributivity.} The coefficient of \(X^k\) in \(f(g+h)\) is
\(\sum_{i+j=k} a_i (b_j + c_j)\), which distributivity in \(F\) splits as
\(\sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j + \sum_{i+j=k} a_i c_j\): the coefficient of
\(X^k\) in \(fg + fh\). The other distributive law follows from this one
and commutativity.

\emph{Identity.} With \(1 = (1,0,0,\dots)\), the \(k\)-th coefficient of
\(1 \cdot f\) is \(\sum_{i+j=k} \indicator[i=0]\, a_j = a_k\), where
\(\indicator[i=0]\) denotes the coefficient sequence of \(1\) (equal to
\(1\) at index \(0\) and \(0\) elsewhere); so \(1 \cdot f = f\).

\emph{Embedding.} Let \(\iota(c) = (c,0,0,\dots)\). The map \(\iota\)
preserves sums coordinatewise, and \(\iota(c)\iota(d)\) has \(k\)-th
coefficient \(cd\) for \(k = 0\) and \(0\) otherwise, so
\(\iota(c)\iota(d) = \iota(cd)\); also \(\iota(1) = 1\). Distinct
constants give distinct sequences, so \(\iota\) is an injective ring
homomorphism (\cref{ringsfields:def-ringhom}) and its image is a subring
of \(F[X]\) isomorphic to \(F\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scalar multiplication]\label{polynomials:rem:scalar}
Multiplying \(f = \sum_i a_i X^i\) by a constant \(c \in F\), viewed as
the constant polynomial \(\iota(c)\), gives the \emph{scalar multiple}
\(cf = \sum_i (c a_i) X^i\). Together with addition, this makes \(F[X]\)
a \emph{vector space} over \(F\)---a structure studied systematically in
the linear-algebra section later in the volume; informally, a system
closed under addition and under scaling by field elements, in which the
monomials \(1, X, X^2, X^3, \dots\) play the role of an infinite
coordinate system (a \emph{basis}). This linear structure is relied on
constantly, for instance whenever we count polynomials of bounded
degree.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Degree}\label{polynomials:sub:degree}

\begin{definition}[Degree, leading coefficient, monic]
\label{polynomials:def:degree}
Let \(f = \sum_i a_i X^i \in F[X]\) be nonzero. The \emph{degree}
\(\deg f\) is the largest index \(n\) with \(a_n \neq 0\); the
coefficient \(a_n\) is the \emph{leading coefficient}
\(\operatorname{lc}(f)\), the term \(a_n X^n\) is the \emph{leading
term}, and \(a_0\) is the \emph{constant term}. A nonzero polynomial is
\emph{monic} if its leading coefficient is \(1\). The constant
polynomials are the elements of \(F\) embedded by
\cref{polynomials:thm:ring}; the nonzero ones have degree \(0\). The zero
polynomial is assigned \(\deg 0 = -\infty\), with the conventions
\(-\infty < n\), \(-\infty + n = -\infty\), and
\(-\infty + (-\infty) = -\infty\) for every \(n \in \Z\). Polynomials of
degree \(1\), \(2\), and \(3\) are called \emph{linear},
\emph{quadratic}, and \emph{cubic} respectively.
\end{definition}

The convention \(\deg 0 = -\infty\) is not pedantry: it makes the degree
formulas below hold without exceptions, which streamlines every later
induction on degree. Induction on degree is thereby set up as a reusable
device for the rest of the volume.

\begin{proposition}[Degree of sums and products]\label{polynomials:prop:degree}
For all \(f, g \in F[X]\),
\begin{equation}\label{polynomials:eq:deg-sum}
\deg(f + g) \;\le\; \max\{\deg f, \deg g\},
\end{equation}
with equality whenever \(\deg f \neq \deg g\), and
\begin{equation}\label{polynomials:eq:deg-prod}
\deg(f g) \;=\; \deg f + \deg g .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If either polynomial is zero, both statements reduce to the
\(-\infty\) conventions of \cref{polynomials:def:degree}. So let \(f\)
and \(g\) be nonzero, of degrees \(m\) and \(n\) with leading
coefficients \(a_m\) and \(b_n\).

\emph{Sum.} The coefficient of \(X^k\) in \(f+g\) is \(a_k + b_k\),
which vanishes for \(k > \max\{m,n\}\); this is
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-sum}. If \(m > n\), the coefficient of
\(X^m\) is \(a_m + 0 = a_m \neq 0\), giving equality (symmetrically for
\(n > m\)). When \(m = n\), cancellation \(a_m + b_m = 0\) can lower the
degree, which is why the sum bound is only an inequality.

\emph{Product.} The coefficient
\(c_{m+n} = \sum_{i+j=m+n} a_i b_j\) has only the surviving term
\(i = m\), \(j = n\): terms with \(i > m\) have \(a_i = 0\), and terms
with \(i < m\) force \(j > n\), so \(b_j = 0\). Hence
\(c_{m+n} = a_m b_n\), and the same argument shows \(c_k = 0\) for
\(k > m+n\). Since \(F\) is a field it has no zero divisors
(\cref{ringsfields:prop-field-domain}), so \(a_m b_n \neq 0\); therefore
\(\deg(fg) = m + n\) and \(\operatorname{lc}(fg) =
\operatorname{lc}(f)\operatorname{lc}(g)\).
\end{proof}

The product formula \eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod} is the first place
the field hypothesis does real work: it required \(a_m b_n \neq 0\), that
is, the absence of zero divisors in \(F\). This single fact has sweeping
consequences for everything that follows, beginning with the next three
corollaries.

\begin{corollary}[{\(F[X]\) is an integral domain}]
\label{polynomials:cor:domain}
The ring \(F[X]\) has no zero divisors: if \(fg = 0\), then \(f = 0\) or
\(g = 0\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If \(f\) and \(g\) are both nonzero, then
\(\deg(fg) = \deg f + \deg g \ge 0\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod}, so \(fg \neq 0\). Contrapositively,
\(fg = 0\) forces \(f = 0\) or \(g = 0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Cancellation]\label{polynomials:cor:cancellation}
Let \(f, g, h \in F[X]\) with \(h \neq 0\). If \(fh = gh\), then
\(f = g\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
From \(fh = gh\) we get \((f - g)h = 0\); since \(h \neq 0\),
\cref{polynomials:cor:domain} forces \(f - g = 0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[{Units of \(F[X]\)}]\label{polynomials:prop:units}
A polynomial \(f \in F[X]\) is a unit---has a multiplicative inverse in
\(F[X]\)---if and only if it is a nonzero constant. Thus
\(F[X]^\times = F^\times\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A nonzero constant \(c\) has the inverse \(c^{-1} \in F \subseteq F[X]\).
Conversely, \(fg = 1\) gives
\(\deg f + \deg g = \deg 1 = 0\) by \eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod};
since degrees of nonzero polynomials are nonnegative,
\(\deg f = \deg g = 0\), and \(f\) is a nonzero constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Associates]\label{polynomials:def:associates}
Two polynomials \(f, g \in F[X]\) are \emph{associates} if \(f = ug\) for
some unit \(u \in F^\times\), that is, if they differ only by a nonzero
scalar factor.
\end{definition}

Every nonzero polynomial \(f\) has exactly one monic associate, obtained
by dividing through by its leading coefficient:
\(\operatorname{lc}(f)^{-1} f\). Choosing the monic representative is the
standard way to pin down \emph{the} greatest common divisor or \emph{the}
irreducible factor uniquely, and we adopt this normalisation below.

\subsection{Division with remainder}\label{polynomials:sub:division}

The arithmetic of \(F[X]\) parallels that of the integers \(\Z\) with
remarkable fidelity: greatest common divisors, a Euclidean algorithm, a
B\'ezout identity, primes (here called irreducibles), and unique
factorisation all reappear. The source of the parallel is a single
statement, division with remainder, in which \emph{degree} plays the role
that absolute value plays in \(\Z\).

\begin{theorem}[Division algorithm]\label{polynomials:thm:division}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\) with \(g \neq 0\). There exist \emph{unique}
polynomials \(q, r \in F[X]\) such that
\[
f = qg + r
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\deg r < \deg g .
\]
We call \(q\) the \emph{quotient} and \(r\) the \emph{remainder}, and
write \(q = f \operatorname{div} g\) and \(r = f \bmod g\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Existence.} Fix \(g\), of degree \(n\) with leading coefficient
\(b_n\), and induct on \(\deg f\). If \(\deg f < n\)---including
\(f = 0\) with \(\deg f = -\infty\)---take \(q = 0\) and \(r = f\).
Otherwise let \(m = \deg f \ge n\), with leading coefficient \(a_m\),
and form
\[
f_1 \;=\; f - \frac{a_m}{b_n}\, X^{m-n}\, g .
\]
The subtracted term has degree \(m\) and leading coefficient
\((a_m/b_n)\, b_n = a_m\), so it cancels the leading term of \(f\):
either \(f_1 = 0\) or \(\deg f_1 < m\). By the induction hypothesis
\(f_1 = q_1 g + r\) with \(\deg r < n\), and then
\(f = \bigl(q_1 + (a_m/b_n) X^{m-n}\bigr) g + r\). The field hypothesis
enters visibly here: division by the leading coefficient \(b_n\)
requires \(b_n^{-1}\) to exist.

\emph{Uniqueness.} Suppose \(f = qg + r = q'g + r'\) with both
remainders of degree \(< n\). Then \((q - q')g = r' - r\). If
\(q \neq q'\), then
\(\deg\bigl((q-q')g\bigr) = \deg(q - q') + n \ge n\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod}, whereas
\(\deg(r' - r) \le \max\{\deg r', \deg r\} < n\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-sum}---a contradiction. Hence \(q = q'\), and
then \(r = f - qg = r'\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Polynomial long division]\label{polynomials:rem:long-division}
The existence proof is constructive, and it is precisely \emph{polynomial
long division}: repeatedly eliminate the current leading term by
subtracting an appropriate monomial multiple of \(g\). Each step strictly
lowers the degree of the running remainder, so the process terminates
after at most \(\deg f - \deg g + 1\) steps. The device is reusable in
both of its guises: as an algorithm, and as the
leading-term-elimination pattern for inductions on degree.

For a concrete instance, divide \(f = X^3 + 2X + 1\) by
\(g = X^2 + 1\) over \(\Q\). Subtracting
\(X \cdot g = X^3 + X\) leaves \(X + 1\), whose degree \(1\) is already
below \(\deg g = 2\); hence \(q = X\) and \(r = X + 1\), and indeed
\[
X^3 + 2X + 1 \;=\; X\,(X^2 + 1) + (X + 1) .
\]
One elimination step suffices here.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Divisibility]\label{polynomials:def:divisibility}
For \(f, g \in F[X]\), we say \(g\) \emph{divides} \(f\), written
\(g \mid f\), if \(f = qg\) for some \(q \in F[X]\); equivalently, for
\(g \neq 0\), if the remainder \(f \bmod g\) is \(0\). In that case
\(g\) is a \emph{divisor} (or \emph{factor}) of \(f\).
\end{definition}
\subsection{Roots and the factor theorem}\label{polynomials:sub:roots}

The destination of this subsection is the central counting bound of the
whole section, stated at once so that the reader knows what the machinery
is for: \emph{a nonzero polynomial of degree \(d\) over a field has at
most \(d\) distinct roots} (\cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound} below).
Everything the volume later builds on polynomials---the cyclicity of the
multiplicative group of a finite field, Lagrange interpolation, the
Schwartz--Zippel principle---is load-bearing on this one fact. To reach
it we must first say what a root is, and that requires making
substitution precise: this is the first place where a polynomial gives
rise to a function.

\begin{definition}[Evaluation and roots]\label{polynomials:def:evaluation}
Let \(f = \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i X^i \in F[X]\) and \(\alpha \in F\). The
\emph{evaluation} of \(f\) at \(\alpha\) is the field element
\[
f(\alpha) \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i \alpha^i \;\in\; F,
\]
obtained by substituting \(\alpha\) for the indeterminate \(X\). An
element \(\alpha \in F\) is a \emph{root} (or \emph{zero}) of \(f\) if
\(f(\alpha) = 0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Evaluation is a ring homomorphism]
\label{polynomials:prop:eval-hom}
Fix \(\alpha \in F\). The evaluation map
\(\operatorname{ev}_\alpha\colon F[X] \to F\), \(f \mapsto f(\alpha)\),
satisfies
\[
(f+g)(\alpha) = f(\alpha) + g(\alpha),
\qquad
(fg)(\alpha) = f(\alpha)\, g(\alpha),
\qquad
1(\alpha) = 1
\]
for all \(f, g \in F[X]\); that is, \(\operatorname{ev}_\alpha\) is a
ring homomorphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Additivity is immediate from the coordinatewise definition of addition.
For multiplicativity, with \(c_k = \sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j\) as in
\cref{polynomials:def:operations},
\[
(fg)(\alpha)
= \sum_{k} c_k \alpha^k
= \sum_{k} \sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j\, \alpha^{i+j}
= \Bigl(\sum_i a_i \alpha^i\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_j b_j \alpha^j\Bigr)
= f(\alpha)\, g(\alpha),
\]
the middle equality because expanding the product of the two finite sums
and grouping terms by \(k = i + j\) produces exactly the double sum. The
formal reason evaluation respects the algebra is that the convolution
defining polynomial multiplication collapses to ordinary multiplication
in \(F\) via \(\alpha^i \alpha^j = \alpha^{i+j}\). Finally
\(1(\alpha) = 1\) by direct substitution.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Remainder theorem; factor theorem]\label{polynomials:thm:factor}
Let \(f \in F[X]\) and \(\alpha \in F\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textup{(Remainder theorem.)} The remainder of \(f\) upon division
  by the linear polynomial \(X - \alpha\) is the constant
  \(f(\alpha)\): there is \(q \in F[X]\) with
  \(f = q\,(X - \alpha) + f(\alpha)\).
\item \textup{(Factor theorem.)} The element \(\alpha\) is a root of
  \(f\) if and only if \((X - \alpha) \mid f\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(1) Divide \(f\) by \(X - \alpha\)
(\cref{polynomials:thm:division}): since \(\deg(X - \alpha) = 1\), the
remainder has degree \(< 1\), hence is a constant \(r \in F\).
Evaluating \(f = q\,(X-\alpha) + r\) at \(\alpha\) via
\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom} gives
\(f(\alpha) = q(\alpha)(\alpha - \alpha) + r = r\).

(2) By (1) and the uniqueness of the remainder,
\((X - \alpha) \mid f\) holds exactly when the remainder \(f(\alpha)\)
is \(0\), that is, when \(\alpha\) is a root of \(f\).
\end{proof}

The factor theorem is an immediate but powerful consequence of the
division algorithm: it converts a statement about \emph{values} (a root)
into a statement about \emph{divisibility} (a linear factor). Iterating
that conversion proves the promised bound.

\begin{theorem}[At most \(d\) roots]\label{polynomials:thm:root-bound}
A nonzero polynomial \(f \in F[X]\) of degree \(d\) has at most \(d\)
distinct roots in \(F\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We induct on \(d = \deg f\). If \(d = 0\), then \(f\) is a nonzero
constant, which evaluates to itself everywhere and so has \(0 \le d\)
roots. Let \(d \ge 1\). If \(f\) has no root in \(F\) the claim holds;
otherwise let \(\alpha\) be a root. By the factor theorem,
\(f = (X - \alpha)\, q\), and \(\deg q = d - 1\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod}. Now let \(\beta \neq \alpha\) be any
other root of \(f\). Evaluating with
\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom},
\[
0 = f(\beta) = (\beta - \alpha)\, q(\beta);
\]
since \(\beta - \alpha \neq 0\) and \(F\) has no zero divisors
(\cref{ringsfields:prop-field-domain}), we get \(q(\beta) = 0\). Every
root of \(f\) other than \(\alpha\) is therefore a root of \(q\), and by
the induction hypothesis \(q\) has at most \(d - 1\) distinct roots.
Hence \(f\) has at most \((d-1) + 1 = d\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why a field is needed]\label{polynomials:rem:field-needed}
Both ingredients of the proof---the absence of zero divisors and the
degree bookkeeping---are essential. Over a ring with zero divisors the
bound fails outright: in \(\Z/8\Z\) the quadratic \(X^2 - 1\) has the
four roots \(\overline{1}, \overline{3}, \overline{5}, \overline{7}\),
since \(1^2 = 1\), \(3^2 = 9 \equiv 1\), \(5^2 = 25 \equiv 1\), and
\(7^2 = 49 \equiv 1 \pmod 8\)---a degree-\(2\) polynomial with four
roots. This is why the polynomial methods underlying Halo~2 require a
field, and indeed a finite field \(\Fp\) or \(\Fq\), beneath them.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Few points determine a low-degree polynomial]
\label{polynomials:cor:agree-implies-equal}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\) both have degree at most \(d\). If
\(f(\alpha) = g(\alpha)\) for \(d + 1\) distinct values
\(\alpha \in F\), then \(f = g\) as polynomials.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The difference \(h = f - g\) has
\(\deg h \le \max\{\deg f, \deg g\} \le d\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-sum} and vanishes at \(d + 1\) distinct
points. Were \(h\) nonzero, it would be a polynomial of degree at most
\(d\) with \(d + 1\) distinct roots, contradicting
\cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}. Hence \(h = 0\), that is, \(f = g\).
\end{proof}

The corollary is the deterministic skeleton of two constructions
developed later in this section: Lagrange interpolation
(\S\ref{polynomials:sub:lagrange}), its constructive converse, and the
Schwartz--Zippel principle (\S\ref{polynomials:sub:sz}), its
probabilistic refinement. The root bound is thus positioned as the
load-bearing fact behind the polynomial identity testing and
interpolation machinery used by the proving system.

\subsection{Greatest common divisors}\label{polynomials:sub:gcd}

Because \(F[X]\) supports division with remainder, the entire theory of
greatest common divisors from elementary number theory
(\S\ref{sec:intmod}) transfers verbatim from \(\Z\) to \(F[X]\), with
degree replacing absolute value as the measure that decreases. We run
through the transfer explicitly, both because the statements are used
constantly and because the polynomial Euclidean algorithm is itself a
tool of the trade.

\begin{definition}[Greatest common divisor]\label{polynomials:def:gcd}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\), not both zero. A \emph{greatest common divisor}
of \(f\) and \(g\) is a polynomial \(d\) such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(d \mid f\) and \(d \mid g\); and
\item every common divisor \(c\) of \(f\) and \(g\) satisfies
  \(c \mid d\).
\end{enumerate}
A greatest common divisor is determined only up to multiplication by a
unit; the unique \emph{monic} one (\cref{polynomials:thm:euclid}) is
singled out and denoted \(\gcd(f,g)\). When \(\gcd(f,g) = 1\), the
polynomials \(f\) and \(g\) are called \emph{coprime} (or
\emph{relatively prime}). Since every polynomial divides \(0\), we have
\(\gcd(f,0) = \operatorname{lc}(f)^{-1} f\) for \(f \neq 0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Euclidean step]\label{polynomials:lem:euclid-step}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\) with \(g \neq 0\), and let \(f = qg + r\) be the
division of \cref{polynomials:thm:division}. Then the pairs \((f,g)\)
and \((g,r)\) have exactly the same common divisors; in particular,
their greatest common divisors coincide.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(c \mid f\) and \(c \mid g\), then \(c \mid (f - qg) = r\); so \(c\)
is a common divisor of \((g,r)\). Conversely, if \(c \mid g\) and
\(c \mid r\), then \(c \mid (qg + r) = f\). The two sets of common
divisors are therefore equal, hence so are their maximal elements under
divisibility.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Euclidean algorithm; B\'ezout's identity]
\label{polynomials:thm:euclid}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\), not both zero. Then \(\gcd(f,g)\) exists and is
unique as a monic polynomial, and there exist \(s, t \in F[X]\) with
\begin{equation}\label{polynomials:eq:bezout}
\gcd(f,g) \;=\; s f + t g .
\end{equation}
Concretely: set \(r_0 = f\) and \(r_1 = g\), and while \(r_i \neq 0\)
divide
\[
r_{i-1} = q_i\, r_i + r_{i+1},
\qquad \deg r_{i+1} < \deg r_i .
\]
The degrees strictly decrease, so some \(r_{N+1} = 0\); the last nonzero
remainder \(r_N\), made monic, is \(\gcd(f,g)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Termination.} If \(r_1 = 0\), the algorithm stops at once with
\(N = 0\) and \(r_N = f \neq 0\). Otherwise the degrees
\(\deg r_1 > \deg r_2 > \dots\) form a strictly decreasing sequence of
nonnegative integers, which cannot continue forever; so some
\(r_{N+1} = 0\) with \(r_N \neq 0\).

\emph{The gcd property.} \Cref{polynomials:lem:euclid-step}, applied at
each division, preserves the \emph{set} of common divisors:
\[
\{\text{common divisors of } (f,g)\}
= \{\text{common divisors of } (r_1,r_2)\}
= \dots
= \{\text{common divisors of } (r_N, 0)\},
\]
and the last set is exactly the set of divisors of \(r_N\), since every
polynomial divides \(0\). Thus \(r_N\) divides both \(f\) and \(g\), and
every common divisor of \(f\) and \(g\) divides \(r_N\): the monic
associate \(\operatorname{lc}(r_N)^{-1} r_N\) is a monic greatest common
divisor.

\emph{Uniqueness.} Two monic greatest common divisors divide each other,
so they differ by a unit \(u \in F^\times\)
(\cref{polynomials:prop:units}); comparing leading coefficients of monic
polynomials forces \(u = 1\).

\emph{B\'ezout.} We show by induction on \(i\) that each remainder
\(r_i\) is an \(F[X]\)-linear combination of \(f\) and \(g\). The bases
\(r_0 = 1 \cdot f + 0 \cdot g\) and \(r_1 = 0 \cdot f + 1 \cdot g\) are
trivial, and the recurrence \(r_{i+1} = r_{i-1} - q_i r_i\) expresses
\(r_{i+1}\) as a combination once \(r_{i-1}\) and \(r_i\) are. Hence
\(r_N = s_0 f + t_0 g\) for some \(s_0, t_0 \in F[X]\); dividing through
by \(\operatorname{lc}(r_N)\) yields \eqref{polynomials:eq:bezout}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Extended Euclidean algorithm]\label{polynomials:rem:eea}
Carrying the linear-combination coefficients along with the remainders,
exactly as in the integer case
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:exteuclid}), produces \(s\) and \(t\)
explicitly alongside the gcd. The standard application is inversion in a
quotient \(F[X]/(m)\) for irreducible \(m\): if \(\gcd(f, m) = 1\), then
\(sf + tm = 1\), so \(s\) is the inverse of \(f\) modulo \(m\). This is
how one divides in the extension fields \(\Fq\) constructed in
\cref{sec:finitefields}. The polynomial version matters both because it
parallels the integer one exactly and because it computes gcds of the
polynomials a proof system manipulates. A caveat on deployed practice:
the curves of Halo~2 and Orchard are defined over \emph{prime} fields
\(\Fp\), not extension fields, and field inversion there uses the
integer extended Euclidean algorithm
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:exteuclid}) or Fermat's little theorem---not
the polynomial algorithm in \(F[X]/(m)\).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[{Euclid's lemma in \(F[X]\)}]
\label{polynomials:cor:euclid-lemma}
Let \(p, f, g \in F[X]\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item If \(\gcd(p, f) = 1\) and \(p \mid fg\), then \(p \mid g\).
\item If \(p\) is irreducible and \(p \mid fg\), then \(p \mid f\) or
  \(p \mid g\).
\end{enumerate}
Here \emph{irreducible} is the polynomial analogue of prime, made
precise in \cref{polynomials:def:irreducible} below.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) Multiplying the B\'ezout identity \(sp + tf = 1\) by \(g\) gives
\(spg + tfg = g\). The polynomial \(p\) divides both terms on the
left---the second because \(p \mid fg\)---hence divides \(g\).

(2) Suppose \(p \nmid f\). The gcd \(\gcd(p, f)\) is a monic divisor of
\(p\) that is not the monic associate of \(p\) (otherwise \(p \mid f\)).
